I get up, usually around 5:45, because the markets open early and I like to be ahead of the curve for my clients. I go into the master bathroom, and we have these overhead LED lights. My wife picked them out during a little remodel we did last year, and they are just... unforgivingly bright. And the second I flick that switch, my eyes just dart straight to the lower right side of my face. \n\nIt’s this... this heaviness. Like the skin has just given up right along the jawbone. If I tilt my head down, even a fraction of an inch, it pools there, making my whole face look completely lopsided. I'll stand there, shaving, and I have to pull the skin tight just to get a clean pass with the razor, and when I let go, it just slumps back down. It definitely feels different from how I looked even, say, just a few years ago. I know I'm 45, I know I'm not a kid anymore, I get that, but this isn't just normal aging. It's like a structural collapse on one side. \n\nAnd to your second point, about others not seeing it... that is exactly what drives me absolutely up the wall. I’ll stand there in the bathroom, and my wife will come in to grab her things, and I’ll point right at it in the mirror. I’ll say, 'Look at this, look at how the shadow catches this droop right here.' And she just sighs and tells me I’m being ridiculous. She says I look exactly like I always have, which is nice of her to say, I guess, but it’s just not true. I can see the geometry of it, the asymmetry. \n\nIt makes me feel like I’m losing my mind because it is so glaringly obvious to me, like a neon sign on my face, and everyone else just acts like I have a perfectly normal jawline. It really makes me wonder if people are just being polite, you know? Because like I said, in my line of work, appearance is trust. If you look tired or like you're physically falling apart, why would someone trust you with their life savings? So yeah, it's just me seeing it, apparently, but I know what I'm looking at. I know it's there.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself checking your face in mirrors or photos frequently, or spending a lot of time trying to fix or hide this part of your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I mean, I really, really do. It's... it's become this whole routine, honestly. Especially before I have to meet with a client. I have this process now. So, say I have a meeting at 10:00 AM. By 9:45, I'm usually up from my desk and I head down the hall to the men's room. The lighting in there is different from my office—it's more fluorescent, kind of harsh, so I feel like it shows everything. I'll stand there at the sink, and I'll lean in close to the mirror, just checking that lower right side. I'll turn my head left, turn it right, trying to see how the shadows fall. \n\nAnd then, even after I leave the bathroom, I'll pull out my phone as I'm walking back to my office. I'll open the front-facing camera—not to take a picture, but just to use it as another mirror, because you know how phone cameras can distort things or show you a different angle. I'll probably check my phone camera three or four times before the client even walks through the door. \n\nAnd the lighting in my office... I've actually started messing with the blinds. I used to just leave them open, let the natural light in, but now I'm constantly adjusting the slats. If the sun is coming in from the left, it casts a shadow right on that sagging spot, so I have to tilt the blinds up, or sometimes I'll even move my chair over a few inches so I'm not sitting directly in the beam of light. \n\nI've also started thinking a lot about my clothes. I'll wear shirts with slightly stiffer, higher collars, hoping it might kind of... prop up the skin a little, or at least draw the eye away from my jawline. When I'm sitting there across from a client, I find myself actively trying to angle my face. Like, I'll try to keep the left side of my face angled slightly more toward them, or I'll rest my chin on my hand, just casually, you know, like I'm really deep in thought about their 401k, but really I'm just trying to physically push the skin back up where it belongs. \n\nIt's exhausting. And the worst part is, it's completely distracting me. Like, my wife and I went to this art gallery opening a couple of weeks ago—one of her friends is a sculptor—and there were all these people there, a lot of them younger, successful-looking folks. And instead of looking at the art or actually listening to the conversations, I kept catching myself staring at the other men there. Specifically the younger financial guys, the ones in their early thirties. I'm just looking at their jawlines, comparing them to mine, thinking, 'Look at how tight his skin is, look at how straight that line is.' I'll be nodding along to a story someone's telling, but in my head, I'm just obsessing over how my face compares to theirs, and worrying if they're noticing my lopsided jaw while I'm standing there. It's just... it's taking up so much space in my head, doctor.\n\nDoctor: Has this concern about your jawline affected your ability to function normally at work, at home, or in social situations?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean, I still get through my work. I haven't dropped the ball on any major accounts or anything like that, thank God, because my clients really depend on me. But... it’s definitely bleeding into things. Like I was telling you about my whole routine before meetings? That all takes time. So lately, I’ve been finding myself slower getting out the door for client appointments. I’ll have my briefcase packed, the prospectuses all printed and ready to go, but I just feel this overwhelming need to do one more check in the hallway mirror, and then maybe adjust my collar again to see if it hides the droop, and suddenly I’m having to hustle just to not be late. \n\nAnd then, when I’m actually sitting there in the appointment... I really hate to admit this, because I pride myself on being incredibly attentive to my clients, but I’m just less present. A client will be talking about their estate planning or their grandkids' college funds—something really deeply important to them—and I’ll realize I completely missed the last two sentences. I missed them because I was too busy calculating the angle of the overhead light in the conference room and wondering if they’re staring at the sag on my jawline. I’m nodding and making the right noises, but my brain is just miles away, entirely focused on my face and how uneven it looks.\n\nAnd socially, it’s... well, it's taking a toll there, too. You know I mentioned that art gallery opening my wife and I went to? Well, there have been a couple more of those recently—her friend the sculptor is very active in the local arts scene—and I’ve actually just skipped them. I’ll make up some excuse, tell my wife I have to review some end-of-quarter reports or that I’m just too exhausted from the market fluctuations that week. But the truth is, I just didn't want to be seen. I couldn't stomach the idea of standing under those harsh gallery lights with all those put-together people looking at me. \n\nAnd the photos... there are always people snapping pictures at those events, for Instagram or the gallery's website or whatever. The thought of a camera catching me off-guard, capturing that lopsided droop from the wrong angle and immortalizing it where anyone could see it... I just can't do it. Not after what happened with those family photos. So I just stay home. I mean, I’m still functioning on a day-to-day basis, I'm still paying the bills, but it just feels like I'm constantly having to navigate my entire life around this... this huge obstacle, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your concern about your jawline has caused you significant distress or has interfered with your daily life, such as avoiding social situations or spending a lot of time trying to cope with these thoughts?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean, exactly. It’s like I was just saying a minute ago, it really is interfering, even though I’m fighting tooth and nail to not let it ruin my life. I still get through my work, you know? I’m still functioning, I’m still managing the portfolios and doing the day-to-day heavy lifting for my firm, but the friction it adds to everything is just... it's immense, doctor. It really is. \n\nTake the mornings, for example. I’ve definitely been slower getting out the door for client meetings. I'll have everything ready, my briefcase packed, my keys in my hand, the car is probably already running in the driveway, and I just get stuck. I get stuck at the hallway mirror doing one last check, trying to adjust my posture or pull at my collar to hide the sagging, and then suddenly I’m looking at my watch and I have to speed all the way across town just to not be late. It's incredibly stressful. \n\nAnd then, once I'm actually there, sitting across from the client... like I mentioned, I’m just less present during some of these appointments. It absolutely kills me to admit that, because my clients deserve a hundred percent of my attention, especially with the market fluctuating the way it has been this quarter, but part of my brain is just entirely hijacked. I'm trying to listen to them talk about their retirement goals, but really I'm just worrying about how my face looks in their office lighting and wondering if they're staring at my jaw. \n\nAnd the social stuff, yeah, it's a huge source of distress. Like those art openings I was telling you about? I’ve skipped a few of them recently, entirely because of this. My wife will be getting dressed up, looking beautiful, and I'll just make up some excuse about being tired from work, but the real reason—the honest truth—is I just didn’t want to be seen. I couldn't handle the thought of standing around under those bright gallery spotlights, or worse, having someone point a camera at me. The thought of being photographed and having that lopsided droop captured on film for everyone to see... I just panic. It’s just easier to stay home, even if it makes me feel miserable and isolated to miss out. So yes, it is causing me a lot of distress. It feels like a second full-time job just trying to manage it all.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like others are intentionally hiding the truth from you about how your face looks, or do you think they’re just not being honest because they don’t want to hurt your feelings?\n\nPatient: No, I... I don't really think that. I don't think there's, like, a coordinated effort to keep the truth from me or anything. It’s not like I think my wife or my clients are looking at me and thinking, 'Oh, wow, his face is a disaster, I better not say anything so I don't hurt his pride.' I don't get that sense at all. \n\nIn my line of work, you know, as a financial advisor, you have to get a pretty good read on people. You have to know when a client is maybe inflating their income a bit, or downplaying how much market risk they can actually stomach. You learn to read the room and pick up on those little micro-expressions. So I feel like if people were actively lying to my face to protect my feelings, I would definitely pick up on it. \n\nI think it’s more just... I don't know, maybe they just don't focus on it the way I do? Or maybe they do notice it, but it's just a fleeting thought for them. Like, maybe a client looks at me across the desk and thinks, 'Huh, his jaw looks a little uneven,' but then two seconds later they're right back to worrying about their capital gains taxes or their retirement timeline. Whereas for me, it's the only thing playing on a loop in my head the entire hour. \n\nTake my wife, for example. She's a wonderful woman, but she is incredibly blunt. She does not sugarcoat things whatsoever. If I put on an ugly tie or a suit that doesn't fit right, she will literally stop me at the door and tell me to go upstairs and change. So when she looks at me and says she honestly doesn't see this massive droop on the right side of my face... I don't think she's lying to be sweet. I really don't. I just... I honestly just get completely baffled as to how she can't see it when it's so glaringly obvious to me in every single mirror. It’s almost like we’re looking at two entirely different reflections. \n\nBut no, to answer your question, I don't think people are hiding the truth from me or pitying me. That sounds a bit too... I don't know, paranoid? And I don't feel paranoid about other people's motives at all. I'm just incredibly frustrated by what I'm seeing on my own face, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain behaviors or thoughts over and over again because of how you perceive your face, like checking mirrors, adjusting your appearance, or going over how you looked in a situation after it's happened?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. It's almost become like a... well, a second job, like I said. The repetition is probably the most exhausting part of all this. Before I even step foot into a client meeting, I have this whole routine that I just can't seem to break away from. I’ll go into the bathroom down the hall from my office—specifically the one on the east side because the overhead bulbs are a little softer—and I'll check the mirror there. But that's never enough to settle my mind. So I go back to my desk and I’ll pull out my smartphone and use the front-facing camera. I must open and close that camera app several times before a meeting, just staring at the screen, analyzing the droop. I'll even get up and adjust the blinds in my office to change the lighting, trying to see how the shadows fall on my jaw from different angles to figure out where the client should sit. I’ll sit there messing with my shirt, changing how my collar sits, maybe unbuttoning the top button to see if it draws the eye away, and I’ll practice angling my face—like tilting my chin up and to the left—so the sagging isn't as obvious when the door opens. \n\nAnd then, the thoughts... the comparisons are just a whole other loop my brain gets stuck in. I'll be right in the middle of a situation, and instead of actually focusing on the conversation, I'm just sizing up everyone else's face. I do it constantly at the firm. I'll catch myself looking at the younger advisors—these kids in their late twenties who just got their Series 7 licenses—and I'm just entirely fixated on how sharp and even their jawlines are compared to mine. \n\nOr, you know, when I do manage to go out, like to those gallery events I mentioned earlier? I'll be standing there, and instead of listening to my wife's friends talk about the sculptures or whatever, I'm just scanning the room, comparing my face to every other man there. I'm just analyzing how their skin sits on their jaw and going over and over in my head how much worse I look than them. It’s a constant, running commentary that I just can't seem to shut off, no matter how hard I try to just be present and focus on the conversation in front of me. It's just a relentless cycle.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about this asymmetry in your jawline more than other aspects of your appearance, and does the thought of it consume more of your attention than other things in your life?\n\nPatient: Oh, without a doubt. It’s... well, to be completely honest with you, it is exactly what brings me in here to see you in the first place. I don't really worry about my hair thinning or my weight or, you know, getting older in general. I mean, I'm forty-five, I know I'm not a kid fresh out of college anymore. But the one thing I just keep getting stuck on, the thing that completely monopolizes my mental bandwidth, is this sagging, uneven look around my jaw and down that one side of my face. \n\nIt really kicked into overdrive recently, which is why it's consuming so much of my attention now compared to other things in my life. We had this family event, and of course, everyone is snapping pictures all day. And later, when I was looking through the shared album online... I just sat there staring at the screen. Seeing myself in all those family photos, caught from all these different angles where I wasn't prepared or posing... it was just devastating. I spent I don't even know how long just zooming in on my jawline in every single picture while everyone else was probably just looking at how nice the scenery was. \n\nAnd the part that really spins me out, the thing I just can't reconcile in my brain, is that despite how glaring it is to me, my wife and my clients say they barely notice anything at all. Like I was saying before about how blunt my wife is, she swears she doesn't see it. But I look at those photos, and it's all I can see. It just overrides everything else I should be focusing on, whether I'm at home or at the firm. I just keep getting stuck on it, over and over, and it's exhausting.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel driven to perform certain behaviors or mental acts, like checking your appearance or comparing yourself to others, in response to the anxiety this facial asymmetry causes you?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, exactly like I was just saying a minute ago... it really is the anxiety driving all of it. It’s like this intense pressure builds up in my chest, and I just *have* to do those things to try and calm it down, even though it never really works. \n\nLike I detailed before, right before those client meetings, the worry just takes over. I feel completely compelled to go check that bathroom mirror, and then I’m back at my desk pulling up my phone camera several times just to check it again. I’m physically getting out of my chair to adjust the lighting with the blinds, I’m messing with my collar to see if it hides things better, I’m practicing how to angle my face... I do all of that because if I don't, the anxiety just completely eats at me. I can't even focus on the financial reports sitting right in front of me until I've gone through that whole routine. \n\nAnd it’s the exact same thing with the comparisons I was telling you about. I feel totally driven to do it. The anxiety makes me catch myself comparing my face to those younger advisors at the firm, or sizing up all the men at those gallery events, instead of actually focusing on the conversation I'm supposed to be having. It’s like the worry forces me to scan the room and measure myself against every single person there. I just can't sit there and be present. I have to look at their jawlines and figure out where I stand. It's exhausting, honestly, having your brain hijack you like that when you're just trying to explain tax-loss harvesting to a client or support your wife at an art show. I just feel so pushed by the worry to keep checking and comparing, over and over again.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like your concern about your jawline is excessive or unreasonable, even if you can't seem to stop thinking about it?\n\nPatient: Well, see, that's the thing... I really don't think it's excessive at all. I know you're asking if I feel like it's unreasonable, and maybe someone else might think so—like my wife, bless her, who just wants me to stop talking about it—but when you're the one actually living with it? No. I don't think it's unreasonable to care about your face falling apart. Especially, you know, in my line of work. I mean, I'm a financial advisor. People are handing over their life savings to me, their retirement portfolios, their kids' 529 college funds. They need to look across the desk and see someone who is completely put together, someone sharp and reliable. \n\nAnd if my face is sagging on one side... if my jawline is all uneven and I look like I'm, I don't know, melting, or recovering from some kind of medical event—which I'm not, my health is perfectly fine otherwise—but if I *look* asymmetrical, it projects this total lack of control. It makes me look tired, or incompetent, or past my prime. So, no, I don't think spending time trying to mitigate that is an overreaction. \n\nI mean, if you think about those family photos I mentioned earlier... if you had seen what I saw in those pictures, the way the afternoon sun caught that droop on the side of my face, you'd probably be doing the exact same thing I am. It's just... it's reality. It's right there. It's right there in the bathroom mirror, it's right there in the front-facing camera on my phone, it's in the photos. So how can it possibly be excessive to worry about something that is a glaring, physical reality? I'm really just trying to manage it, you know? I'm just trying to survive these client meetings and these gallery events without looking like a complete mess. So, unreasonable? Honestly, no. I think it's a completely rational response to a very real, very obvious problem with my face.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever notice physical symptoms like sweating, shaking, or a fast heartbeat when you're really focused on how your face looks before a meeting or when you're out in public?\n\nPatient: I mean... no, not really. I wouldn't say I get physical symptoms like that. It's not like a panic attack, you know? I'm not sitting there in my office before a big portfolio review, sweating through my suit or shaking like a leaf. I've seen people have actual panic attacks—my sister used to get them when we were in our twenties, and she'd be practically hyperventilating, heart pounding out of her chest, the whole nine yards. But for me, it's nothing like that. \n\nIt's more just... this intense, heavy mental pressure. Like a buzzing in my head that I just can't turn off until I've checked the mirror or fixed the lighting in my office. I don't feel my heart racing when I'm at one of my wife's gallery shows and I'm comparing myself to the other guys there. It's just this overwhelming distraction, this sinking feeling that everyone is looking at the droop in my jawline and judging me for it. But physically? No. My hands are steady when I'm handing over the financial reports. I'm not breaking out in a cold sweat. It's all just happening in my head, this constant, exhausting loop of worrying about how I look and trying to manage it. So, yeah, I don't get the shaking or the sweating or any of that physical stuff you mentioned. It's just the anxiety itself, if that makes sense.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. The provided profile only has S067, S068, functional impairment. Since the patient already talked about S067, S068, and insight (in the previous turn), the doctor is now asking about the lack of control over the concern (obsessional nature). None of the items specifically match 'loss of control' beyond what S067 broadly covers, but S067 was already used for the chief complaint. Actually, 'None' is the safest choice since it's asking about control/intrusiveness specifically, which isn't explicitly detailed in the S067 description (it just says 'Preoccupation...'). Let's use 'None'."}

Well, that's exactly it, isn't it? The control factor. Or, I should say, the complete and utter lack of it. Because, believe me, I try. I try so hard to just put it out of my mind. It's incredibly frustrating, to feel like your own brain is working against you. No. I don't think it's unreasonable to care about your face falling apart. Especially, you know, in my line of work. I mean, I'm a financial advisor. People are handing over their life savings to me, their retirement portfolios, their kids' 529 college funds. They need to look across the desk and see someone who is completely put together, someone sharp and reliable. \n\nAnd if my face is sagging on one side... if my jawline is all uneven and I look like I'm, I don't know, melting, or recovering from some kind of medical event—which I'm not, my health is perfectly fine otherwise—but if I *look* asymmetrical, it projects this total lack of control. It makes me look tired, or incompetent, or past my prime. It's just... it's reality. It's right there. It's right there in the bathroom mirror, it's right there in the front-facing camera on my phone, it's in the photos. So how can it possibly be excessive to worry about something that is a glaring, physical reality? I'm really just trying to manage it, you know? I'm just trying to survive these client meetings and these gallery events without looking like a complete mess. So, unreasonable? Honestly, no. I think it's a completely rational response to a very real, very obvious problem with my face.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever notice physical symptoms like sweating, shaking, or a fast heartbeat when you're really focused on how your face looks before a meeting or when you're out in public?\n\nPatient: I mean... no, not really. I wouldn't say I get physical symptoms like that. It's not like a panic attack, you know? I'm not sitting there in my office before a big portfolio review, sweating through my suit or shaking like a leaf. I've seen people have actual panic attacks—my sister used to get them when we were in our twenties, and she'd be practically hyperventilating, heart pounding out of her chest, the whole nine yards. But for me, it's nothing like that. \n\nIt's more just... this intense, heavy mental pressure. Like a buzzing in my head that I just can't turn off until I've checked the mirror or fixed the lighting in my office. I don't feel my heart racing when I'm at one of my wife's gallery shows and I'm comparing myself to the other guys there. It's just this overwhelming distraction, this sinking feeling that everyone is looking at the droop in my jawline and judging me for it. But physically? No. My hands are steady when I'm handing over the financial reports. I'm not breaking out in a cold sweat. It's all just happening in my head, this constant, exhausting loop of worrying about how I look and trying to manage it. So, yeah, I don't get the shaking or the sweating or any of that physical stuff you mentioned. It's just the anxiety itself, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your concern about your jawline is something you can't control, even though you try not to focus on it?\n\nPatient: Well, that's exactly it, isn't it? I'll be sitting there at my desk, looking at a spreadsheet, trying to analyze a client's asset allocation, and I'll tell myself, 'Okay, just focus on the numbers. Focus on the yield curve. I practically order myself to stop thinking about it. \n\nBut it's like... it's like trying not to think about a pink elephant, you know? I'll be mid-sentence with a client, explaining the tax implications of a Roth conversion, and suddenly my brain is just screaming at me about the droop on the left side of my jaw. It's completely involuntary. I can't stop the thoughts from coming, and once they're there, I can't just wave a magic wand and make them disappear. \n\nAnd it's not for lack of trying, I promise you. No. My hands are steady when I'm handing over the financial reports. I'm not breaking out in a cold sweat. It's all just happening in my head, this constant, exhausting loop of worrying about how I look and trying to manage it. So, yeah, I don't get the shaking or the sweating or any of that physical stuff you mentioned. It's just the anxiety itself, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your concern about your jawline is something you can't control, even though you try not to focus on it?\n\nPatient: Well, that's exactly it, isn't it? The control factor. Or, I should say, the complete and utter lack of it. Because, believe me, I try. I try so hard to just put it out of my mind. I'll be sitting there at my desk, looking at a spreadsheet, trying to analyze a client's asset allocation, and I'll tell myself, 'Okay, just focus on the numbers. Focus on the yield curve. Don't think about the asymmetry. Don't think about how your face is sagging.' I practically order myself to stop thinking about it. \n\nBut it's like... it's like trying not to think about a pink elephant, you know? The harder I try to push the thought away, the louder it gets. It just creeps right back in. I'll be mid-sentence with a client, explaining the tax implications of a Roth conversion, and suddenly my brain is just screaming at me about the droop on the left side of my jaw. It's completely involuntary. I can't stop the thoughts from coming, and once they're there, I can't just wave a magic wand and make them disappear. \n\nAnd it's not for lack of trying, I promise you. I've tried distracting myself, I've tried taking deep breaths, I've even tried snapping a rubber band on my wrist—I read that somewhere, that it's supposed to help break the thought cycle. But nothing works. The concern is just always there, hovering in the background, waiting to hijack my attention the second I let my guard down. It's incredibly frustrating, to feel like your own brain is working against you. To know that you should be focusing on your job, or your wife, or the conversation you're having, but instead you're entirely consumed by this one, glaring flaw that you have absolutely no control over. So, yes, to answer your question, it feels completely out of my hands. The harder I try not to focus on it, the more it just takes over everything."
